Company brief

KONGSBERG DEFENCE & AEROSPACE AS is a private limited company operating in Produksjon av våpen og ammunisjon. Latest accounts (2025): revenue NOK 14.0bn, annual result NOK 1.9bn. Annual accounts available for 2011-2025 (15 years). Detailed records only available as PDFs. Revenue shrinking (-17.7% YoY). Recent activity

public_post company_site Our history - KONGSBERG - an international technology group KONGSBERG has a long and proud history. From its starting point in Norway in 1814, to the international corporation of today, KONGSBERG has been an innovative technology environment. Through our foresight and ability to adapt, KONGSBERG has survived through changing times and markets, celebrating our 200-year anniversary in 2014, now stronger than ever. The history of “Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk” dates back to a time before Norway's Constitution was established in 1814. Following the dissolution of the union with Denmark, the need arose to establish a strong defence to ensure Norway's independence and security. At this time, the silver mines in Kongsberg experienced a decline, but the town already had a significant pool of expertise and established industry through “Jernverket”. It was in this context that Poul Steenstrup, one of the members of the Constitutional Assembly at Eidsvoll, founded "Kongsberg Vaabenfabrik" on March 20, 1814, with approval from Prince Regent Christian Frederik. Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk started producing rifles for the Norwegian Armed Forces. Increasing deliveries throughout the 19th century lead to the big international breakthrough in 1888, when director Ole Hermann Johannes Krag and gunsmith Erik Jørgensen introduced the prototype of their new rifle. The rifle was bought by the Danish army in 1889. Three years later, the Krag-Jørgensen became world-famous when the United States decided to equip its soldiers with the rifle. Krag-Jørgensen was to dominate the production at Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk until the end of the first world war in 1918. Modern times In the start of 1918 the company also introduced production for the civil market. Three years later, the first tools from Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk were launched on the market. These included adjustable spanners, twist drills, chisels, planes, spade handles, blades for circular saws and a range of pliers – to name but a few. As always, these products were top quality and won the company a gold medal during the International Exposition in Barcelona in 1929. The company's new production plans for the civil market covered much more than tools however. In the early 1920s, the company launched axle shafts and components for cars and ships, sledges with skis as runners, complete bicycles, dining room furniture, tool cabinets, irons and even a couple of prototypes for sewing machines. Whaling guns became particularly popular for export. In the 1930s, the threat of war dominated Europe once again and Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk manufactured amongst others antiaircraft guns under license. During the Second World War, from 1940 to 1945, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was controlled by the Nazi German occupants, whom were unsatisfied with the factory unable to meet expectations due to poor access to raw materials and resistance among factory workers. Industrial leader After the end of the Second World War, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was designated as one of key companies in the rebuilding and developing of Norwegian industry and in 1953 the owner, The Norwegian Parliament, initiated a large scale modernization of the company. This was motivated by the desire to build a national, high tech defence industry that could meet the Norwegian armed forces' needs and contribute to NATO. The establishment of the Norwegian Armed Forces' research institute at Kjeller in 1947 was part of the strategy. The Norwegian authorities wanted a national industrial partner for the Armed Forces to realise the technology. From the 1950s to the present, this collaboration has resulted in a number of defence systems such as the Penguin missile, the HUGIN underwater vessel and the Naval Strike Missile. Innovative breakthroughs In the 1970s, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was divided into separate divisions as they emerged with specific responsibilities to; maintain aircraft parts, produce automotive parts, develop and produce gas turbines, subsea oil installations, maritime equipment, drawing machines, CNCs and defence equipment. Dynamic positioning and subsea installations were revolutionary technologies, which gave the city of Kongsberg a technological edge that still prevails today. New start The year 1987 was a turning point in the company’s history. Although the level of innovation was high, it was not profitable. The Norwegian state, which owned all the shares, decided to sell all divisions except the defence division which continued under the name Norsk Forsvarsteknologi as. In 1995 the company reclaim Kongsberg in the new company identity; Kongsberg Gruppen ASA (KONGSBERG). In 1997, KONGSBERG acquired Kongsberg Maritime AS which together with newly established Kongsberg Defence & Aerospace AS (formerly named Kongsberg Gruppen ASA) became two business areas. Global player At the turn of the century, KONGSBERG seized the opportunity when the need for a remote controlled weapon solution for vehicles arrived. KONGSBERG's solution proved effective and the U.S. defence for

   "claim_span": "Our history - KONGSBERG - an international technology group\n\nKONGSBERG has a long and proud history.\nFrom its starting point in Norway in 1814, to the international corporation of today, KONGSBERG has been an innovative technology environment.\nThrough our foresight and ability to adapt, KONGSBERG has survived through changing times and markets, celebrating our 200-year anniversary in 2014, now stronger than ever.\nThe history of “Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk” dates back to a time before Norway's Constitution was established in 1814. Following the dissolution of the union with Denmark, the need arose to establish a strong defence to ensure Norway's independence and security.\nAt this time, the silver mines in Kongsberg experienced a decline, but the town already had a significant pool of expertise and established industry through “Jernverket”. It was in this context that Poul Steenstrup, one of the members of the Constitutional Assembly at Eidsvoll, founded \"Kongsberg Vaabenfabrik\" on March 20, 1814, with approval from Prince Regent Christian Frederik.\nKongsberg Våpenfabrikk started producing rifles for the Norwegian Armed Forces. Increasing deliveries throughout the 19th century lead to the big international breakthrough in 1888, when director Ole Hermann Johannes Krag and gunsmith Erik Jørgensen introduced the prototype of their new rifle.\nThe rifle was bought by the Danish army in 1889.        "main_text_excerpt": "This is KONGSBERG\nKONGSBERG protects people and critical infrastructure – from deep sea to space. Through innovation, collaboration and determination, we develop technologies and solutions to serve the defence, security and surveillance markets.\nAbout us\nWe combine military and civilian expertise to drive rapid innovation for defence, research, and commercial applications.\nOur defence and security solutions include strike missiles, remote weapon stations, air defence systems, command and control systems and digital remote towers for customers worldwide. Our solutions help strengthen countries' security and defence capabilities.\nKONGSBERG also serves the ocean space with technology – from sensors to autonomous systems – to ensure sustainable management of marine resources, monitor climate change and critical infrastructure, and safeguard national security.\nKONGSBERG delivers to every part of the space value chain – from launch services and production of small satellites to downloading and managing satellite data.\nThe company was founded in 1814 in the town of Kongsberg, Norway. Today, we are more than 7,500 employees in about 20 countries. The company is headquartered in Norway and listed on the Oslo Stock Exchange. The Norwegian government controls about 50.01 percent of the shares in KONGSBERG.\nThrough innovation and state-of-the-art industrial production, we create jobs and value for our suppliers, partners, customers and owners.\nWe protect people and planet by innovating technology today, for a better tomorrow.\nThree strong divisions\nKONGSBERG consists of three divisions:\n- Defence Systems\n- Missiles & Aerostructures\n- Discovery\nDefence Systems\nThis division focuses on land systems, air and coastal defence, naval systems and advanced defence solutions. It includes core defence capabilities such as integrated defence systems, weapon platforms and advanced operational solutions. The division carries significant responsibility for deliveries within highly regulated defence environments and plays a central role in meeting growing demand from NATO and allied markets.\nMissiles & Aerostructures\nThis division brings together missile programmes, aerostructures and maintenance capabilities. It includes both subsonic and supersonic missile programmes, aerostructures production and maintenance of airborne platforms. It also has a dedicated Emerging Business unit to support innovation and new growth areas. The division consolidates advanced manufacturing and programme execution under one structure, strengthening end-to-end accountability across complex value chains.\nDiscovery\nThis division integrates the former business area Kongsberg Discovery with Space and Surveillance activities. It combines capabilities in underwater technology, sensors, satellite systems and surveillance, creating a cohesive environment for dual-use and cross-domain innovation. Most civilian-oriented activities are gathered within this division. This allows these businesses to operate with the flexibility required to compete in international civilian markets, without being subject to defence-specific rules and processes that apply elsewhere in the company. At the same time, the division benefits from close collaboration with defence and space activities, strengthening technology development across domains.\nOwnership structure\nKongsberg Gruppen ASA is listed on the Oslo Stock Exchange and is subject to Norwegian securities legislation and stock exchange regulations. The Norwegian state owns 50.004 per cent of the shares in the company.\nMore about the company\nSustainability and corporate social responsibility\nSustainability and corporate social responsibility are important for KONGSBERG and are an integral part of our strategy. We shall conduct our business in a sustainable and accountable manner, and we must carry out our corporate social responsibility in accordance with the applicable expectations of society. This gives KONGSBERG the necessary ‘licence to operate’ in order to execute our business.\nThe UN has defined 17 sustainability development goals the world should reach by 2030. Several of these goals can only be achieved through innovation and the sensible application of technology. To KONGSBERG, this involves business opportunities in several markets viewed in the light of our broad technology and skills platform.\nKONGSBERG has acceded to the UN Global Compact initiative. We support and respect international human and employee rights such as the UN’s Universal Declaration of Human Rights, the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child, ILO Core Conventions and the OECD Guidelines for Multinational Enterprises. KONGSBERG uses the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) guidelines for the voluntary reporting of sustainable development.\nValue creation\nKONGSBERG is an active value creator in the areas and countries where we are present. Our presence creates significant effects that extend beyond our own business area. First and foremost, we create v",

       "main_text_excerpt": "Following the demerger and separate listing of Kongsberg Maritime, Kongsberg Gruppen ASA (‘KONGSBERG’) is now dedicated to developing the technology and solutions required to protect people and critical infrastructure – from deep sea to space.\n“The decision to separate the two companies was taken with an eye on future market opportunities. To create more value in a rapidly changing world, where security and sustainability are expected to dominate in the coming decades,” said Eivind Reiten, Chair of the Board of KONGSBERG Gruppen ASA.\n“KONGSBERG will be better positioned to navigate and grasp opportunities in its markets with a Board of Directors and executive leadership that are solely focused on defence, security and related matters,” said Reiten.\n                        \nI am proud to lead KONGSBERG into the future, together with more than 7,500 highly skilled and motivated colleagues. Our mission – to protect people and planet – has never been more appropriate and right.\nThis is why we exist – and why I believe we are positioned to continue to grow.\nEirik Lie, President and Chief Executive Officer of KONGSBERG.\nLie succeeds Geir Håøy, who led KONGSBERG as President and CEO from 2016 till 2026.\n“Together, we combine a unique blend of military and civilian expertise to drive rapid innovation for defence, research, and commercial applications.        "content_sha256": "a4e87ace1d14884511f2c8b03fa1c3c43b38b8bfd26c64b14375f2e0de97124d",
       "main_text_excerpt": "KONGSBERG has a long and proud history.\nFrom its starting point in Norway in 1814, to the international corporation of today, KONGSBERG has been an innovative technology environment.\nThrough our foresight and ability to adapt, KONGSBERG has survived through changing times and markets, celebrating our 200-year anniversary in 2014, now stronger than ever.\nThe history of “Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk” dates back to a time before Norway's Constitution was established in 1814. Following the dissolution of the union with Denmark, the need arose to establish a strong defence to ensure Norway's independence and security.\nAt this time, the silver mines in Kongsberg experienced a decline, but the town already had a significant pool of expertise and established industry through “Jernverket”. It was in this context that Poul Steenstrup, one of the members of the Constitutional Assembly at Eidsvoll, founded \"Kongsberg Vaabenfabrik\" on March 20, 1814, with approval from Prince Regent Christian Frederik.\nKongsberg Våpenfabrikk started producing rifles for the Norwegian Armed Forces. Increasing deliveries throughout the 19th century lead to the big international breakthrough in 1888, when director Ole Hermann Johannes Krag and gunsmith Erik Jørgensen introduced the prototype of their new rifle.\nThe rifle was bought by the Danish army in 1889. Three years later, the Krag-Jørgensen became world-famous when the United States decided to equip its soldiers with the rifle.\nKrag-Jørgensen was to dominate the production at Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk until the end of the first world war in 1918.\nModern times\nIn the start of 1918 the company also introduced production for the civil market.\nThree years later, the first tools from Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk were launched on the market. These included adjustable spanners, twist drills, chisels, planes, spade handles, blades for circular saws and a range of pliers – to name but a few.\nAs always, these products were top quality and won the company a gold medal during the International Exposition in Barcelona in 1929.\nThe company's new production plans for the civil market covered much more than tools however. In the early 1920s, the company launched axle shafts and components for cars and ships, sledges with skis as runners, complete bicycles, dining room furniture, tool cabinets, irons and even a couple of prototypes for sewing machines. Whaling guns became particularly popular for export.\n                            \nIn the 1930s, the threat of war dominated Europe once again and Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk manufactured amongst others antiaircraft guns under license.\nDuring the Second World War, from 1940 to 1945, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was controlled by the Nazi German occupants, whom were unsatisfied with the factory unable to meet expectations due to poor access to raw materials and resistance among factory workers.\nIndustrial leader\nAfter the end of the Second World War, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was designated as one of key companies in the rebuilding and developing of Norwegian industry and in 1953 the owner, The Norwegian Parliament, initiated a large scale modernization of the company.\nThis was motivated by the desire to build a national, high tech defence industry that could meet the Norwegian armed forces' needs and contribute to NATO.\nThe establishment of the Norwegian Armed Forces' research institute at Kjeller in 1947 was part of the strategy. The Norwegian authorities wanted a national industrial partner for the Armed Forces to realise the technology.\nFrom the 1950s to the present, this collaboration has resulted in a number of defence systems such as the Penguin missile, the HUGIN underwater vessel and the Naval Strike Missile.\nInnovative breakthroughs\nIn the 1970s, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was divided into separate divisions as they emerged with specific responsibilities to; maintain aircraft parts, produce automotive parts, develop and produce gas turbines, subsea oil installations, maritime equipment, drawing machines, CNCs and defence equipment.\nDynamic positioning and subsea installations were revolutionary technologies, which gave the city of Kongsberg a technological edge that still prevails today.\nNew start\nThe year 1987 was a turning point in the company’s history. Although the level of innovation was high, it was not profitable. The Norwegian state, which owned all the shares, decided to sell all divisions except the defence division which continued under the name Norsk Forsvarsteknologi as.\nIn 1995 the company reclaim Kongsberg in the new company identity; Kongsberg Gruppen ASA (KONGSBERG). In 1997, KONGSBERG acquired Kongsberg Maritime AS which together with newly established Kongsberg Defence & Aerospace AS (formerly named Kongsberg Gruppen ASA) became two business areas.\nGlobal player\nAt the turn of the century, KONGSBERG seized the opportunity when the need for a remote controlled weapon solution for vehicles arrived. KONGSBERG's solution proved effective and the U.S. defence for",

       "main_text_excerpt": "A recent marine autonomy trial off the coast of Plymouth brought together new observing capabilities, including KONGSBERG scientific echosounder technology and a miniaturized holographic plankton imaging sensor from the University of Plymouth aboard the National Oceanography Centre’s Autosub Long Range AUV Boaty McBoatface to study marine ecosystems at oceanographic fronts - dynamic boundaries between different water masses important for marine life.\n- \n                        \nText:KONGSBERG Photo:Peter Miller\nTaking scientific echosounders to autonomous platforms\nAs ocean research moves towards increasingly autonomous operations, integrating proven scientific instruments onto new platforms opens up new possibilities for sustained and efficient ecosystem monitoring.\nDuring a two-week trial in Plymouth, KONGSBERG supported the integration of an EK80 scientific echosounder with two transducers into the National Oceanography Centre’s Autosub Long Range autonomous underwater vehicle, ALR1500, better known as Boaty McBoatface.\nThe National Oceanography Centre's ALR1500 vessel, aka \"Boaty MycBoatface\", being lowered into the water. (Photo: Lillian Lieber)\nFrom left - Dr Lilian Lieber, Dr Peter Miller and Dr Yang Yang heading out to see during the two-week trial (Photo: Lillian Lieber)\nMembers of the trial team at Turnchapel Wharf, which played an instrumental role in making the trial deployments possible (Photo: Dr Yang Yang)\nDr Lilian Lieber and the ALR1500 during the two-week trial off the coast of Plymouth (Photo: Peter Miller)\nKONGSBERG Ocean Science Product Advisor Robert Sørhagen joined the team in Plymouth, providing specialist expertise and support throughout the integration and trials. “This trial is a great example of what can be achieved when scientific expertise, autonomous technology and advanced ocean instrumentation come together,” Sørhagen said. “Working closely with the University of Plymouth and the National Oceanography Centre, we are bringing proven scientific echosounder technology to autonomous platforms and enabling new ways to study marine ecosystems.”\n                        \nThis trial is a great example of what can be achieved when scientific expertise, autonomous technology and advanced ocean instrumentation come together\nRobert Sørhagen, Product Advisor Ocean Science, KONGSBERG\nCombined with other onboard sensors, the EK80 was used to observe fish and zooplankton in the water column alongside measurements of the physical and biogeochemical conditions that influence their distribution. Early observations included a large fish school detected by the EK80, followed by a pod of common dolphins swimming alongside the AUV.\nBuilding a fuller picture of the ocean\nThe trial forms part of Frontline, a £3.5 million consortium within the UK's NERC ECOFlow programme. The project is exploring how ocean fronts influence marine ecosystems and how these dynamic environments could be affected by the expansion of floating offshore wind in the Celtic Sea.\nBy combining autonomous platforms with scientific echosounders, satellite observations, and other ocean monitoring technologies, researchers can collect complementary measurements over wider areas and for longer periods. The approach can help build the ecosystem understanding needed as offshore wind developments move into deeper and more complex marine environments.\nFor KONGSBERG, the collaboration reflects our ambition to support a sustainable ocean by bringing versatile scientific echosounder technology to new platforms and applications. Working closely with our customers at the University of Plymouth and the National Oceanography Centre also supports their transition towards autonomous platforms for future ecosystem assessments.\nThe trial is an important step towards more extensive scientific surveys planned in the Celtic Sea in 2027. It successfully demonstrates the wider potential of autonomous ocean observation for research in offshore wind regions across Europe and highlights the combination of space-borne and in situ observations to build a fuller picture of dynamic ocean features.\nThe Frontline collaboration brings together the University of Plymouth, Heriot-Watt University, Plymouth Marine Laboratory, the National Oceanography Centre, SINTEF Ocean, HiDef Aerial Surveying Ltd and KONGSBERG. The Plymouth trial was also supported by Turnchapel Wharf, Zero USV and Thales.\nAbout the Frontline project\nFrontline is investigating how floating offshore wind (FLOW) development could affect ocean fronts and marine biodiversity – from plankton to predators – in the Celtic Sea.\nThe project combines autonomous underwater vehicle (AUV) surveys, satellite remote sensing, aerial surveys, seabird tracking, fisheries data and stakeholder engagement to investigate how ocean fronts shape ecological processes and fishing activity across the Celtic Sea, and how these may change with the expansion of large-scale FLOW development.\nThe University of Plymouth is develo",

       "main_text_excerpt": "Turning defence investment into operational capabilities requires more than technology. At the Atlantic Future Forum in Oslo, KONGSBERG highlighted how governments, industry and operational users can work together to deliver solutions for the North Atlantic and High North.\n- \n                        \nText:Njål Frilseth / KONGSBERG Photo:NATO\nRepresentatives from 29 countries gathered in Oslo on 11–12 November for the Atlantic Future Forum, hosted aboard the Royal Navy aircraft carrier HMS Prince of Wales at the invitation of Norway and the United Kingdom. The event featured contributions from more than 50 political, military, government and industry leaders, focusing on international security, technology and industrial partnerships.f\nThe carrier arrived following its participation in NATO's Arctic Sentry and other allied activities in Norwegian waters. British carrier aircraft had operated alongside Norwegian F-35 fighter jets, while the Norwegian frigate KNM Fridtjof Nansen joined the carrier group, demonstrating practical cooperation between allied forces.\n                            \nNATO Secretary General Mark Rutte emphasised the importance of maritime security for international trade, energy supplies and vital communications.\n                            \nDescribing the North Atlantic as “the bridge that connects the two continents in our Alliance,” he also called on the defence industry to increase production, accelerate innovation and work together to meet growing demand.\nFor KONGSBERG, closer collaboration between governments and industry is essential to translating defence investments into operational capabilities.\n“Agreeing on shared ambitions is important, but what matters is what we deliver together. Involving industry early allows us to combine expertise, develop technology and build the capacity needed to deliver,” said Eirik Lie, President and CEO of KONGSBERG.\nConnecting technology and expertise in the High North\nLie participated in a panel discussion on Northern Europe, the High North and Arctic, addressing the challenges of protecting critical underwater infrastructure and the importance of clear responsibilities between authorities, operators and industry.\nCamilla Kiss, Executive Vice President and President of Discovery at KONGSBERG, also joined a discussion on securing the North Atlantic and High North at the International Sea Power Conference, held aboard the carrier alongside the forum.\n                        \nWe have the technology and experience to operate in harsh environments, from space to beneath Arctic ice. Now we need governments, users, and industry to work more closely to turn investment into capability at sea.\nCamilla Kiss, Executive Vice President and President of Discovery at KONGSBERG\nProtecting critical maritime infrastructure requires technologies and organisations to work together. Underwater sensors, autonomous systems, satellite surveillance and secure communications can provide vital information, but effective protection also depends on clear responsibilities and the ability to respond.\nKiss highlighted KONGSBERG's joint High North Proposal with BAE Systems, presented to the relevant Norwegian and British authorities to explore closer cooperation on maritime security and the protection of critical underwater infrastructure.\nShe also pointed to the EU-funded HiAOOS research project, where KONGSBERG contributes ocean observation technology, supported by the Norwegian Coast Guard, to improve understanding of Arctic conditions.\nFrom industrial partnerships to delivery\nNorway and the United Kingdom have longstanding industrial relationships and complementary strengths in maritime and defence technology.\nNorway's decision to acquire British Type 26 frigates represents more than a major defence procurement programme. It provides a foundation for long-term industrial collaboration, creating opportunities for joint technology development, maintenance and support, while bringing British and Norwegian industry closer together.\n                            \nIn a further example of this cooperation, UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting announced at the forum that the United Kingdom would procure three Norwegian standardised offshore support vessels, with an option for three more, under the Lunna House Agreement.\n                            \nThe ships are intended to support Royal Navy mine-hunting operations using autonomous and uncrewed systems. The announcement illustrates how shared capability requirements can lead to joint programmes that combine maritime expertise, technology and long-term industrial activity.\nKONGSBERG already works with British customers and industrial partners across several areas, including the supply of Naval Strike Missiles to the Royal Navy. These relationships provide a foundation for further joint development, production and long-term support.\nThe forum's concluding statement also emphasised the need to increase defence production, accelerate innova",

The Norwegian authorities wanted a national industrial partner for the Armed Forces to realise the technology.\nFrom the 1950s to the present, this collaboration has resulted in a number of defence systems such as the Penguin missile, the HUGIN underwater vessel and the Naval Strike Missile.\nInnovative breakthroughs\nIn the 1970s, Kongsberg Våpenfabrikk was divided into separate divisions as they emerged with specific responsibilities to; maintain aircraft parts, produce automotive parts, develop and produce gas turbines, subsea oil installations, maritime equipment, drawing machines, CNCs and defence equipment.\nDynamic positioning and subsea installations were revolutionary technologies, which gave the city of Kongsberg a technological edge that still prevails today.\nNew start\nThe year 1987 was a turning point in the company’s history. Although the level of innovation was high, it was not profitable. The Norwegian state, which owned all the shares, decided to sell all divisions except the defence division which continued under the name Norsk Forsvarsteknologi as.\nIn 1995 the company reclaim Kongsberg in the new company identity; Kongsberg Gruppen ASA (KONGSBERG). In 1997, KONGSBERG acquired Kongsberg Maritime AS which together with newly established Kongsberg Defence & Aerospace AS (formerly named Kongsberg Gruppen ASA) became two business areas.\nGlobal player\nAt the turn of the century, KONGSBERG seized the opportunity when the need for a remote controlled weapon solution for vehicles arrived.
